[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 1.0 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"77800b","wall_time_seconds":0.2884651,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2504,"output":"\\r\\n# minGPT\\r\\n\\r\\n![mingpt](mingpt.jpg)\\r\\n\\r\\nA PyTorch re-implementation of [GPT](https://github.com/openai/gpt-2), both training and inference. minGPT tries to be small, clean, interpretable and educational, as most of the currently available GPT model implementations can a bit sprawling. GPT is not a complicated model and this implementation is appropriately about 300 lines of code (see [mingpt/model.py](mingpt/model.py)). All that\'s going on is that a sequence of indices feeds into a [Transformer](https://arxiv.org/abs/1706.03762), and a probability distribution over the next index in the sequence comes out. The majority of the complexity is just being clever with batching (both across examples and over sequence length) for efficiency.\\r\\n\\r\\n**note (Jan 2023)**: though I may continue to accept and change some details, minGPT is in a semi-archived state. For more recent developments see my rewrite [nanoGPT](https://github.com/karpathy/nanoGPT). Basically, minGPT became referenced across a wide variety of places (notebooks, blogs, courses, books, etc.) which made me less willing to make the bigger changes I wanted to make to move the code forward. I also wanted to change the direction a bit, from a sole focus on education to something that is still simple and hackable but has teeth (reproduces medium-sized industry benchmarks, accepts some tradeoffs to gain runtime efficiency, etc).\\r\\n\\r\\nThe minGPT library is three files: [mingpt/model.py](mingpt/model.py) contains the actual Transformer model definition, [mingpt/bpe.py](mingpt/bpe.py) contains a mildly refactored Byte Pair Encoder that translates between text and sequences of integers exactly like OpenAI did in GPT, [mingpt/trainer.py](mingpt/trainer.py) is (GPT-independent) PyTorch boilerplate code that trains the model. Then there are a number of demos and projects that use the library in the `projects` folder:\\r\\n\\r\\n- `projects/adder` trains a GPT from scratch to add numbers (inspired by the addition section in the GPT-3 paper)\\r\\n- `projects/chargpt` trains a GPT to be a character-level language model on some input text file\\r\\n- `demo.ipynb` shows a minimal usage of the `GPT` and `Trainer` in a notebook format on a simple sorting example\\r\\n- `generate.ipynb` shows how one can load a pretrained GPT2 and generate text given some prompt\\r\\n\\r\\n### Library Installation\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you want to `import mingpt` into your project:\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\ngit clone https://github.com/karpathy/minGPT.git\\r\\ncd minGPT\\r\\npip install -e .\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n### Usage\\r\\n\\r\\nHere\'s how you\'d instantiate a GPT-2 (124M param version):\\r\\n\\r\\n```python\\r\\nfrom mingpt.model import GPT\\r\\nmodel_config = GPT.get_default_config()\\r\\nmodel_config.model_type = \'gpt2\'\\r\\nmodel_config.vocab_size = 50257 # openai\'s model vocabulary\\r\\nmodel_config.block_size = 1024  # openai\'s model block_size (i.e. input context length)\\r\\nmodel = GPT(model_config)\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nAnd here\'s how you\'d train it:\\r\\n\\r\\n```python\\r\\n# your subclass of torch.utils.data.Dataset that emits example\\r\\n# torch LongTensor of lengths up to 1024, with integers from [0,50257)\\r\\ntrain_dataset = YourDataset()\\r\\n\\r\\nfrom mingpt.trainer import Trainer\\r\\ntrain_config = Trainer.get_default_config()\\r\\ntrain_config.learning_rate = 5e-4 # many possible options, see the file\\r\\ntrain_config.max_iters = 1000\\r\\ntrain_config.batch_size = 32\\r\\ntrainer = Trainer(train_config, model, train_dataset)\\r\\ntrainer.run()\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nSee `demo.ipynb` for a more concrete example.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Unit tests\\r\\n\\r\\nCoverage is not super amazing just yet but:\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\npython -m unittest discover tests\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n### todos\\r\\n\\r\\n- add gpt-2 finetuning demo on arbitrary given text file\\r\\n- add dialog agent demo\\r\\n- better docs of outcomes for existing projects (adder, chargpt)\\r\\n- add mixed precision and related training scaling goodies\\r\\n- distributed training support\\r\\n- reproduce some benchmarks in projects/, e.g. text8 or other language modeling\\r\\n- proper logging instead of print statement amateur hour haha\\r\\n- i probably should have a requirements.txt file...\\r\\n- it should be possible to load in many other model weights other than just gpt2-\\\\*\\r\\n\\r\\n### References\\r\\n\\r\\nCode:\\r\\n\\r\\n- [openai/gpt-2](https://github.com/openai/gpt-2) has the model definition in TensorFlow, but not the training code\\r\\n- [openai/image-gpt](https://github.com/openai/image-gpt) has some more modern gpt-3 like modification in its code, good reference as well\\r\\n- [huggingface/transformers](https://github.com/huggingface/transformers) has a [language-modeling example](https://github.com/huggingface/transformers/tree/master/examples/pytorch/language-modeling). It is full-featured but as a result also somewhat challenging to trace. E.g. some large functions have as much as 90% unused code behind various branching statements that is unused in the default setting of simple language modeling\\r\\n\\r\\nPapers + some implementation notes:\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Improving Language Understanding by Generative Pre-Training (GPT-1)\\r\\n\\r\\n- Our model largely follows the original transformer work\\r\\n- We trained a 12-layer decoder-only transformer with masked self-attention heads (768 dimensional states and 12 attention heads). For the position-wise feed-forward networks, we used 3072 dimensional inner states.\\r\\n- Adam max learning rate of 2.5e-4. (later GPT-3 for this model size uses 6e-4)\\r\\n- LR decay: increased linearly from zero over the first 2000 updates and annealed to 0 using a cosine schedule\\r\\n- We train for 100 epochs on minibatches of 64 randomly sampled, contiguous sequences of 512 tokens.\\r\\n- Since layernorm is used extensively throughout the model, a simple weight initialization of N(0, 0.02) was sufficient\\r\\n- bytepair encoding (BPE) vocabulary with 40,000 merges\\r\\n- residual, embedding, and attention dropouts with a rate of 0.1 for regularization.\\r\\n- modified version of L2 regularization proposed in (37), with w = 0.01 on all non bias or gain weights\\r\\n- For the activation function, we used the Gaussian Error Linear Unit (GELU).\\r\\n- We used learned position embeddings instead of the sinusoidal version proposed in the original work\\r\\n- For finetuning: We add dropout to the classifier with a rate of 0.1. learning rate of 6.25e-5 and a batchsize of 32. 3 epochs. We use a linear learning rate decay schedule with warmup over 0.2% of training. λ was set to 0.5.\\r\\n- GPT-1 model is 12 layers and d_model 768, ~117M params\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Language Models are Unsupervised Multitask Learners (GPT-2)\\r\\n\\r\\n- LayerNorm was moved to the input of each sub-block, similar to a pre-activation residual network\\r\\n- an additional layer normalization was added after the final self-attention block.\\r\\n- modified initialization which accounts for the accumulation on the residual path with model depth is used. We scale the weights of residual layers at initialization by a factor of 1/√N where N is the number of residual layers. (weird because in their released code i can only find a simple use of the old 0.02... in their release of image-gpt I found it used for c_proj, and even then only for attn, not for mlp. huh. https://github.com/openai/image-gpt/blob/master/src/model.py)\\r\\n- the vocabulary is expanded to 50,257\\r\\n- increase the context size from 512 to 1024 tokens\\r\\n- larger batchsize of 512 is used\\r\\n- GPT-2 used 48 layers and d_model 1600 (vs. original 12 layers and d_model 768). ~1.542B params\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Language Models are Few-Shot Learners (GPT-3)\\r\\n\\r\\n- GPT-3: 96 layers, 96 heads, with d_model of 12,288 (175B parameters).\\r\\n- GPT-1-like: 12 layers, 12 heads, d_model 768 (125M)\\r\\n- We use the same model and architecture as GPT-2, including the modified initialization, pre-normalization, and reversible tokenization described therein\\r\\n- we use alternating dense and locally banded sparse attention patterns in the layers of the transformer, similar to the Sparse Transformer\\r\\n- we always have the feedforward layer four times the size of the bottleneck layer, dff = 4 ∗ dmodel\\r\\n- all models use a context window of nctx = 2048 tokens.\\r\\n- Adam with β1 = 0.9, β2 = 0.95, and eps = 10−8\\r\\n- All models use weight decay of 0.1 to provide a small amount of regularization. (NOTE: GPT-1 used 0.01 I believe, see above)\\r\\n- clip the global norm of the gradient at 1.0\\r\\n- Linear LR warmup over the first 375 million tokens. Then use cosine decay for learning rate down to 10% of its value, over 260 billion tokens.\\r\\n- gradually increase the batch size linearly from a small value (32k tokens) to the full value over the first 4-12 billion tokens of training, depending on the model size.\\r\\n- full 2048-sized time context window is always used, with a special END OF DOCUMENT token delimiter\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Generative Pretraining from Pixels (Image GPT)\\r\\n\\r\\n- When working with images, we pick the identity permutation πi = i for 1 ≤ i ≤ n, also known as raster order.\\r\\n- we create our own 9-bit color palette by clustering (R, G, B) pixel values using k-means with k = 512.\\r\\n- Our largest model, iGPT-XL, contains L = 60 layers and uses an embedding size of d = 3072 for a total of 6.8B parameters.\\r\\n- Our next largest model, iGPT-L, is essentially identical to GPT-2 with L = 48 layers, but contains a slightly smaller embedding size of d = 1536 (vs 1600) for a total of 1.4B parameters.\\r\\n- We use the same model code as GPT-2, except that we initialize weights in the layerdependent fashion as in Sparse Transformer (Child et al., 2019) and zero-initialize all projections producing logits.\\r\\n- We also train iGPT-M, a 455M parameter model with L = 36 and d = 1024\\r\\n- iGPT-S, a 76M parameter model with L = 24 and d = 512 (okay, and how many heads? looks like the Github code claims 8)\\r\\n- When pre-training iGPT-XL, we use a batch size of 64 and train for 2M iterations, and for all other models we use a batch size of 128 and train for 1M iterations.\\r\\n- Adam with β1 = 0.9 and β2 = 0.95\\r\\n- The learning rate is warmed up for one epoch, and then decays to 0\\r\\n- We did not use weight decay because applying a small weight decay of 0.01 did not change representation quality.\\r\\n- iGPT-S lr 0.003\\r\\n- No dropout is used.\\r\\n\\r\\n### License\\r\\n\\r\\nMIT\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"d4d979","wall_time_seconds":0.2874508,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":3521,"output":"\\r\\n# nanoGPT\\r\\n\\r\\n![nanoGPT](assets/nanogpt.jpg)\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n**Update Nov 2025** nanoGPT has a new and improved cousin called [nanochat](https://github.com/karpathy/nanochat). It is very likely you meant to use/find nanochat instead. nanoGPT (this repo) is now very old and deprecated but I will leave it up for posterity.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\nThe simplest, fastest repository for training/finetuning medium-sized GPTs. It is a rewrite of [minGPT](https://github.com/karpathy/minGPT) that prioritizes teeth over education. Still under active development, but currently the file `train.py` reproduces GPT-2 (124M) on OpenWebText, running on a single 8XA100 40GB node in about 4 days of training. The code itself is plain and readable: `train.py` is a ~300-line boilerplate training loop and `model.py` a ~300-line GPT model definition, which can optionally load the GPT-2 weights from OpenAI. That\'s it.\\r\\n\\r\\n![repro124m](assets/gpt2_124M_loss.png)\\r\\n\\r\\nBecause the code is so simple, it is very easy to hack to your needs, train new models from scratch, or finetune pretrained checkpoints (e.g. biggest one currently available as a starting point would be the GPT-2 1.3B model from OpenAI).\\r\\n\\r\\n## install\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\npip install torch numpy transformers datasets tiktoken wandb tqdm\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nDependencies:\\r\\n\\r\\n- [pytorch](https://pytorch.org) <3\\r\\n- [numpy](https://numpy.org/install/) <3\\r\\n-  `transformers` for huggingface transformers <3 (to load GPT-2 checkpoints)\\r\\n-  `datasets` for huggingface datasets <3 (if you want to download + preprocess OpenWebText)\\r\\n-  `tiktoken` for OpenAI\'s fast BPE code <3\\r\\n-  `wandb` for optional logging <3\\r\\n-  `tqdm` for progress bars <3\\r\\n\\r\\n## quick start\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you are not a deep learning professional and you just want to feel the magic and get your feet wet, the fastest way to get started is to train a character-level GPT on the works of Shakespeare. First, we download it as a single (1MB) file and turn it from raw text into one large stream of integers:\\r\\n\\r\\n```sh\\r\\npython data/shakespeare_char/prepare.py\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nThis creates a `train.bin` and `val.bin` in that data directory. Now it is time to train your GPT. The size of it very much depends on the computational resources of your system:\\r\\n\\r\\n**I have a GPU**. Great, we can quickly train a baby GPT with the settings provided in the [config/train_shakespeare_char.py](config/train_shakespeare_char.py) config file:\\r\\n\\r\\n```sh\\r\\npython train.py config/train_shakespeare_char.py\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you peek inside it, you\'ll see that we\'re training a GPT with a context size of up to 256 characters, 384 feature channels, and it is a 6-layer Transformer with 6 heads in each layer. On one A100 GPU this training run takes about 3 minutes and the best validation loss is 1.4697. Based on the configuration, the model checkpoints are being written into the `--out_dir` directory `out-shakespeare-char`. So once the training finishes we can sample from the best model by pointing the sampling script at this directory:\\r\\n\\r\\n```sh\\r\\npython sample.py --out_dir=out-shakespeare-char\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nThis generates a few samples, for example:\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\nANGELO:\\r\\nAnd cowards it be strawn to my bed,\\r\\nAnd thrust the gates of my threats,\\r\\nBecause he that ale away, and hang\'d\\r\\nAn one with him.\\r\\n\\r\\nDUKE VINCENTIO:\\r\\nI thank your eyes against it.\\r\\n\\r\\nDUKE VINCENTIO:\\r\\nThen will answer him to save the malm:\\r\\nAnd what have you tyrannous shall do this?\\r\\n\\r\\nDUKE VINCENTIO:\\r\\nIf you have done evils of all disposition\\r\\nTo end his power, the day of thrust for a common men\\r\\nThat I leave, to fight with over-liking\\r\\nHasting in a roseman.\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nlol  `¯\\\\_(ツ)_/¯`. Not bad for a character-level model after 3 minutes of training on a GPU. Better results are quite likely obtainable by instead finetuning a pretrained GPT-2 model on this dataset (see finetuning section later).\\r\\n\\r\\n**I only have a macbook** (or other cheap computer). No worries, we can still train a GPT but we want to dial things down a notch. I recommend getting the bleeding edge PyTorch nightly ([select it here](https://pytorch.org/get-started/locally/) when installing) as it is currently quite likely to make your code more efficient. But even without it, a simple train run could look as follows:\\r\\n\\r\\n```sh\\r\\npython train.py config/train_shakespeare_char.py --device=cpu --compile=False --eval_iters=20 --log_interval=1 --block_size=64 --batch_size=12 --n_layer=4 --n_head=4 --n_embd=128 --max_iters=2000 --lr_decay_iters=2000 --dropout=0.0\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nHere, since we are running on CPU instead of GPU we must set both `--device=cpu` and also turn off PyTorch 2.0 compile with `--compile=False`. Then when we evaluate we get a bit more noisy but faster estimate (`--eval_iters=20`, down from 200), our context size is only 64 characters instead of 256, and the batch size only 12 examples per iteration, not 64. We\'ll also use a much smaller Transformer (4 layers, 4 heads, 128 embedding size), and decrease the number of iterations to 2000 (and correspondingly usually decay the learning rate to around max_iters with `--lr_decay_iters`). Because our network is so small we also ease down on regularization (`--dropout=0.0`). This still runs in about ~3 minutes, but gets us a loss of only 1.88 and therefore also worse samples, but it\'s still good fun:\\r\\n\\r\\n```sh\\r\\npython sample.py --out_dir=out-shakespeare-char --device=cpu\\r\\n```\\r\\nGenerates samples like this:\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\nGLEORKEN VINGHARD III:\\r\\nWhell\'s the couse, the came light gacks,\\r\\nAnd the for mought you in Aut fries the not high shee\\r\\nbot thou the sought bechive in that to doth groan you,\\r\\nNo relving thee post mose the wear\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nNot bad for ~3 minutes on a CPU, for a hint of the right character gestalt. If you\'re willing to wait longer, feel free to tune the hyperparameters, increase the size of the network, the context length (`--block_size`), the length of training, etc.\\r\\n\\r\\nFinally, on Apple Silicon Macbooks and with a recent PyTorch version make sure to add `--device=mps` (short for \\"Metal Performance Shaders\\"); PyTorch then uses the on-chip GPU that can *significantly* accelerate training (2-3X) and allow you to use larger networks. See [Issue 28](https://github.com/karpathy/nanoGPT/issues/28) for more.\\r\\n\\r\\n## reproducing GPT-2\\r\\n\\r\\nA more serious deep learning professional may be more interested in reproducing GPT-2 results. So here we go - we first tokenize the dataset, in this case the [OpenWebText](https://openwebtext2.readthedocs.io/en/latest/), an open reproduction of OpenAI\'s (private) WebText:\\r\\n\\r\\n```sh\\r\\npython data/openwebtext/prepare.py\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nThis downloads and tokenizes the [OpenWebText](https://huggingface.co/datasets/openwebtext) dataset. It will create a `train.bin` and `val.bin` which holds the GPT2 BPE token ids in one sequence, stored as raw uint16 bytes. Then we\'re ready to kick off training. To reproduce GPT-2 (124M) you\'ll want at least an 8X A100 40GB node and run:\\r\\n\\r\\n```sh\\r\\ntorchrun --standalone --nproc_per_node=8 train.py config/train_gpt2.py\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nThis will run for about 4 days using PyTorch Distributed Data Parallel (DDP) and go down to loss of ~2.85. Now, a GPT-2 model just evaluated on OWT gets a val loss of about 3.11, but if you finetune it it will come down to ~2.85 territory (due to an apparent domain gap), making the two models ~match.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you\'re in a cluster environment and you are blessed with multiple GPU nodes you can make GPU go brrrr e.g. across 2 nodes like:\\r\\n\\r\\n```sh\\r\\n# Run on the first (master) node with example IP 123.456.123.456:\\r\\ntorchrun --nproc_per_node=8 --nnodes=2 --node_rank=0 --master_addr=123.456.123.456 --master_port=1234 train.py\\r\\n# Run on the worker node:\\r\\ntorchrun --nproc_per_node=8 --nnodes=2 --node_rank=1 --master_addr=123.456.123.456 --master_port=1234 train.py\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nIt is a good idea to benchmark your interconnect (e.g. iperf3). In particular, if you don\'t have Infiniband then also prepend `NCCL_IB_DISABLE=1` to the above launches. Your multinode training will work, but most likely _crawl_. By default checkpoints are periodically written to the `--out_dir`. We can sample from the model by simply `python sample.py`.\\r\\n\\r\\nFinally, to train on a single GPU simply run the `python train.py` script. Have a look at all of its args, the script tries to be very readable, hackable and transparent. You\'ll most likely want to tune a number of those variables depending on your needs.\\r\\n\\r\\n## baselines\\r\\n\\r\\nOpenAI GPT-2 checkpoints allow us to get some baselines in place for openwebtext. We can get the numbers as follows:\\r\\n\\r\\n```sh\\r\\n$ python train.py config/eval_gpt2.py\\r\\n$ python train.py config/eval_gpt2_medium.py\\r\\n$ python train.py config/eval_gpt2_large.py\\r\\n$ python train.py config/eval_gpt2_xl.py\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nand observe the following losses on train and val:\\r\\n\\r\\n| model | params | train loss | val loss |\\r\\n| ------| ------ | ---------- | -------- |\\r\\n| gpt2 | 124M         | 3.11  | 3.12     |\\r\\n| gpt2-medium | 350M  | 2.85  | 2.84     |\\r\\n| gpt2-large | 774M   | 2.66  | 2.67     |\\r\\n| gpt2-xl | 1558M     | 2.56  | 2.54     |\\r\\n\\r\\nHowever, we have to note that GPT-2 was trained on (closed, never released) WebText, while OpenWebText is just a best-effort open reproduction of this dataset. This means there is a dataset domain gap. Indeed, taking the GPT-2 (124M) checkpoint and finetuning on OWT directly for a while reaches loss down to ~2.85. This then becomes the more appropriate baseline w.r.t. reproduction.\\r\\n\\r\\n## finetuning\\r\\n\\r\\nFinetuning is no different than training, we just make sure to initialize from a pretrained model and train with a smaller learning rate. For an example of how to finetune a GPT on new text go to `data/shakespeare` and run `prepare.py` to download the tiny shakespeare dataset and render it into a `train.bin` and `val.bin`, using the OpenAI BPE tokenizer from GPT-2. Unlike OpenWebText this will run in seconds. Finetuning can take very little time, e.g. on a single GPU just a few minutes. Run an example finetuning like:\\r\\n\\r\\n```sh\\r\\npython train.py config/finetune_shakespeare.py\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nThis will load the config parameter overrides in `config/finetune_shakespeare.py` (I didn\'t tune them much though). Basically, we initialize from a GPT2 checkpoint with `init_from` and train as normal, except shorter and with a small learning rate. If you\'re running out of memory try decreasing the model size (they are `{\'gpt2\', \'gpt2-medium\', \'gpt2-large\', \'gpt2-xl\'}`) or possibly decreasing the `block_size` (context length). The best checkpoint (lowest validation loss) will be in the `out_dir` directory, e.g. in `out-shakespeare` by default, per the config file. You can then run the code in `sample.py --out_dir=out-shakespeare`:\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\nTHEODORE:\\r\\nThou shalt sell me to the highest bidder: if I die,\\r\\nI sell thee to the first; if I go mad,\\r\\nI sell thee to the second; if I\\r\\nlie, I sell thee to the third; if I slay,\\r\\nI sell thee to the fourth: so buy or sell,\\r\\nI tell thee again, thou shalt not sell my\\r\\npossession.\\r\\n\\r\\nJULIET:\\r\\nAnd if thou steal, thou shalt not sell thyself.\\r\\n\\r\\nTHEODORE:\\r\\nI do not steal; I sell the stolen goods.\\r\\n\\r\\nTHEODORE:\\r\\nThou know\'st not what thou sell\'st; thou, a woman,\\r\\nThou art ever a victim, a thing of no worth:\\r\\nThou hast no right, no right, but to be sold.\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nWhoa there, GPT, entering some dark place over there. I didn\'t really tune the hyperparameters in the config too much, feel free to try!\\r\\n\\r\\n## sampling / inference\\r\\n\\r\\nUse the script `sample.py` to sample either from pre-trained GPT-2 models released by OpenAI, or from a model you trained yourself. For example, here is a way to sample from the largest available `gpt2-xl` model:\\r\\n\\r\\n```sh\\r\\npython sample.py \\\\\\r\\n    --init_from=gpt2-xl \\\\\\r\\n    --start=\\"What is the answer to life, the universe, and everything?\\" \\\\\\r\\n    --num_samples=5 --max_new_tokens=100\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you\'d like to sample from a model you trained, use the `--out_dir` to point the code appropriately. You can also prompt the model with some text from a file, e.g. ```python sample.py --start=FILE:prompt.txt```.\\r\\n\\r\\n## efficiency notes\\r\\n\\r\\nFor simple model benchmarking and profiling, `bench.py` might be useful. It\'s identical to what happens in the meat of the training loop of `train.py`, but omits much of the other complexities.\\r\\n\\r\\nNote that the code by default uses [PyTorch 2.0](https://pytorch.org/get-started/pytorch-2.0/). At the time of writing (Dec 29, 2022) this makes `torch.compile()` available in the nightly release. The improvement from the one line of code is noticeable, e.g. cutting down iteration time from ~250ms / iter to 135ms / iter. Nice work PyTorch team!\\r\\n\\r\\n## todos\\r\\n\\r\\n- Investigate and add FSDP instead of DDP\\r\\n- Eval zero-shot perplexities on standard evals (e.g. LAMBADA? HELM? etc.)\\r\\n- Finetune the finetuning script, I think the hyperparams are not great\\r\\n- Schedule for linear batch size increase during training\\r\\n- Incorporate other embeddings (rotary, alibi)\\r\\n- Separate out the optim buffers from model params in checkpoints I think\\r\\n- Additional logging around network health (e.g. gradient clip events, magnitudes)\\r\\n- Few more investigations around better init etc.\\r\\n\\r\\n## troubleshooting\\r\\n\\r\\nNote that by default this repo uses PyTorch 2.0 (i.e. `torch.compile`). This is fairly new and experimental, and not yet available on all platforms (e.g. Windows). If you\'re running into related error messages try to disable this by adding `--compile=False` flag. This will slow down the code but at least it will run.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor some context on this repository, GPT, and language modeling it might be helpful to watch my [Zero To Hero series](https://karpathy.ai/zero-to-hero.html). Specifically, the [GPT video](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kCc8FmEb1nY) is popular if you have some prior language modeling context.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor more questions/discussions feel free to stop by **#nanoGPT** on Discord:\\r\\n\\r\\n[![](https://dcbadge.vercel.app/api/server/3zy8kqD9Cp?compact=true&style=flat)](https://discord.gg/3zy8kqD9Cp)\\r\\n\\r\\n## acknowledgements\\r\\n\\r\\nAll nanoGPT experiments are powered by GPUs on [Lambda labs](https://lambdalabs.com), my favorite Cloud GPU provider. Thank you Lambda labs for sponsoring nanoGPT!\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 0.8 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"b44ca9","wall_time_seconds":0.2116028,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1519,"output":"# GitHub and X compilation records what was actually read\\r\\n\\r\\nCompiled 2026-10-08 against the [video prompts](../karpathy-video-2026-10-08/prompts.md). This is partial Prompt 2 execution, not a declaration that the entire collected corpus has been understood.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Coverage is explicit\\r\\n\\r\\nThe [file and post ledger](repo-x-semantic-coverage.jsonl) inventories 1,215 physical GitHub files including six collector metadata files, from 54 repository directories and 13 gist directories. It inventories 2,072 unique own-author X post IDs after primary-text selection across original posts, replies and the latest frozen supplement. The 111 foreign-author rows remain distinct and are not attributed to Karpathy.\\r\\n\\r\\n- Thirteen named GitHub documents/code files were read completely in this batch: autoresearch README and program; micrograd README and engine; minbpe README and exercise; rendergit README; LLM Council README; makemore README; LLM101n README; build-nanogpt README; minGPT README; nanoGPT README.\\r\\n- Eleven new GitHub source pages capture distinct named documents. Existing autoresearch/micrograd README pages remain authoritative for those two already compiled sources.\\r\\n- One hundred fifty-seven captured X primary texts were read completely: 45 recent topical matches, 12 additional relevant posts, and the earliest 100 remaining texts under 1,200 characters (2023-01-01 through 2023-02-19). Sixteen source pages summarize selected substantial posts. Some of the chronological posts are empty, joke replies or social acknowledgments and do not provide actionable engineering rules. Reading a captured text does not certify the collector captured the full live post, linked media or thread.\\r\\n- The remaining 1,202 GitHub files and 1,915 own-author X texts have explicit `not_read` status. No file inventory, hash pass, keyword filter or first-paragraph extraction substitutes for semantic reading. No gist body was read in this subtask; the existing LLM Wiki source page is owned by the parent task.\\r\\n- GitHub dataset/generated/vendor classifications are tentative filename/path triage labels pending content review. The word count includes training corpora, website bundles and other payload; it is not 4.9 million words of authored beliefs. Preserve these files but do not derive personal rules from them.\\r\\n\\r\\nEach file hash covers original bytes. Each X hash covers the selected captured primary-text field in UTF-8, not the full ledger. Per-ID alternate text hashes and source paths are recorded. Longest text selection is a retrieval choice, not conflict adjudication. Foreign quote text is excluded from these attributed conclusions.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Supported patterns and qualifications\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Measure against a real baseline with controlled comparison.** [Autoresearch instructions](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/repos/autoresearch-program.md) require the first run to establish a baseline and protect evaluation. [Reported agent experiment failures](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/x/experiment-design-failures.md) identify absent baselines, ablations and runtime/FLOP controls. Independent project instructions and a separate experiential post support this pattern.\\r\\n2. **Prefer simple inspectable mechanisms, then add complexity.** [Minbpe](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/repos/minbpe-readme.md) emphasizes readable hackable code, [its exercise](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/repos/minbpe-exercise.md) builds capabilities stepwise, and [agent coding notes](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/x/claude-coding-notes.md) recommend a likely-correct naive algorithm before optimization. The exercise and README from one project alone would not establish two independent examples.\\r\\n3. **Do not buy small metric gains with uncontrolled cost or complexity.** [Autoresearch](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/repos/autoresearch-program.md) names the complexity criterion; [objective and simplicity](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/x/objective-and-simplicity.md) and [two-metric acceptance](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/x/two-metric-acceptance.md) explain runtime/memory/quality tradeoffs. Fixed-time and loss-plus-time policies are different valid experiment designs; do not collapse them into one universal formula.\\r\\n4. **Expose assumptions and give observable success criteria.** [Claude coding notes](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/x/claude-coding-notes.md) criticize silent assumptions and recommend tests-first and success criteria. [Explicit delegation](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/x/explicit-delegation.md) says to understand the agent\'s tools and actions. These are two different dated posts, not independent controlled studies.\\r\\n5. **Keep memory inspectable, local and portable.** [Knowledge bases](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/x/llm-knowledge-bases.md) describes raw-to-wiki, indexed retrieval, filed answers and lint. [Explicit local memory](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/x/explicit-local-memory.md) explains user ownership and interchangeable AI. The related [idea-file post](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/x/idea-files.md) describes the gist and is not an independent instance of deployment success.\\r\\n\\r\\nThese patterns are inputs to the seven-rule derivation, not seven completed rules. The parent task must corroborate teaching claims in distinct lecture/blog sources. Do not fabricate independent support for predictions, deliberately broken examples, or platform verification merely to match a video\'s labels.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Pages added in this batch\\r\\n\\r\\n[Autoresearch program](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/repos/autoresearch-program.md), [micrograd engine](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/repos/micrograd-engine.md), [minbpe README](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/repos/minbpe-readme.md), [minbpe exercise](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/repos/minbpe-exercise.md), [rendergit](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/repos/rendergit-readme.md), [LLM Council](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/repos/llm-council-readme.md), [makemore](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/repos/makemore-readme.md), [LLM101n](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/repos/llm101n-readme.md), [build nanoGPT](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/repos/build-nanogpt-readme.md).\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"926636","wall_time_seconds":0.2517135,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":96,"output":"autoresearch-results.md\\r\\nclaude-coding-notes.md\\r\\ndependency-risk.md\\r\\nempirical-setting.md\\r\\nexperiment-design-failures.md\\r\\nexplicit-delegation.md\\r\\nexplicit-local-memory.md\\r\\nidea-files.md\\r\\ninspectable-running-code.md\\r\\nllm-knowledge-bases.md\\r\\nobjective-and-simplicity.md\\r\\nreproduction-limit.md\\r\\nresearch-community.md\\r\\nteacher-contribution.md\\r\\ntwo-metric-acceptance.md\\r\\nvoice-context.md\\r\\n"}'}]
